ParlaMint-ES-GA_2018-12-04-DSPG094.u1	Good morning. The session is open. The agenda is published in the Official Gazette of Parliament, so we do not proceed to its reading. Debate taken into account by the G.P. of the Galego Nationalist Block, amending Law 6/2017, of December 12, of the ports of Galicia
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2018-12-04-DSPG094.u2	For the presentation of the bill, Mrs. Prado Cores has the floor.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2018-12-04-DSPG094.u3	Good morning. A year ago, the People's Party alone passed Galicia's first Harbour Law. A law of waiting ports that did not respond to expectations, for a law of ports in a country like ours, like Galicia, had to solve multiple problems. That first port law was a missed opportunity to fix many of these pending problems, derived from strategic infrastructures in a country like Galicia, where the sea is vital. The People's Party passed that law alone, from virtually turned back to everyone; a privatizing law of strategic economic and productive activity. It was clear that the real priority was ideological orientation and politics of privatization and business benefit. The goal was not the public service, the goal was unique and exclusively the business, business dynamics, the search for profit instead of cooperation between administrations and rather the public benefit. This port law has been developed from turned backs to the sea sector, from turned backs to neighbouring, and above all from the municipalities where these infrastructures are installed. A clearly recalibrated target, a mercantilist target, the goal of cashing out the city halls. Lack of total dialogue, and also with total discretion. Therefore, in this port law, 122 harbour structures are maintained with their co-responsive spaces inserted into the urban plots of the municipalities; where abusive rates are applied by the use of these public spaces; where intense citizenship activity is carried out in most cases. And they are also maintained as port ownership spaces that, by all logic, should and should be municipal ownership, because they are clearly integrated into the urban plot, into the neighborhood dynamics, and would result in the improvement of the quality of life of neighbors and neighbors becoming municipal ownership. That's why the BNG presents an amendment to that Ports Act 6/2017, of December 12, on a very timely issue: as far as these rates apply to Galician ports are concerned to municipalities and non-profit entities and the need for these spaces, which are owned by Ports but which are fully embedded in the urban plot and is where most of the sport and entertainment activities of the municipalities are carried out, become municipal ownership. And also in the sense that a part of the sanctioning regime refers to the activity of bathing and sport fishing is left to no effect. Many of the municipalities on the shores of the sea have experienced over time many changes in their backs, sometimes for natural and other effects — most of them — by human intervention. These lines have come back at times, but — as I have just said — they are most of the time advanced, modifying the setting of the coast. Many of the land won by the sea has ceased to have a function linked to maritime uses — at the boat level, the cargo and discharge, the marine exploitation — to have a clearly urban use; and therefore, they have remained under the municipal dynamics rather than the dynamics of port uses. At present, and within the urban hulls, there are areas that, if at some point they were coastal, have now ceased to have such character, but they continue to have consideration of maritime-terrestrial areas and are therefore under the jurisdiction of Galician ports. The passage of time has consolidated a situation that had to be resolved, since urban activities and neighborhood activities are carried out in these spaces that are owned by another Administration, of Galician Ports. There is therefore a situation we call it dual. The administration responsible for collecting fees from these spaces is not responsible for the provision of services, the maintenance of the state of the streets, the collection of waste, its urbanization in a very important part. And this dichotomy — which is the most important one — is harming the neighbours and neighbours, who check how there exists, in most cases, abandonment by the Galician Ports of the land they own, preventing, for their part, proper use by the municipality, which, by ensuring the real needs of the neighborhood, assumes management and costs that are not their own. At the same time, there is another reality with regard to the situation of the hotel. We have found that there are host institutions that pay fees for having in their establishment their terrace to the ports of Galicia, and on the sidewalk opposite there are other host settlements that pay their terraces to the Town Hall. We found that there is a difference of 2, 3, 4 and up to 5 times higher than those paid to Galician ports in relation to those paid to the municipalities. So we found ourselves with a regulation of use of two administrations, just passing a street through, with a obvious comparative distress, and with this proposal that we make today at BNG, this situation would be resolved. These spaces are also used for all kinds of activities organized by non-profit entities. As I have just said, in many municipalities there are spaces that are owned by Ports and they are the only spaces that exist in them for sporting, playful or partying activities, with which there are many non-profit entities that do all kinds of activities there — I insist — sports or parties, and that they also have to request authorization from Ports, like the Town Councils, and Ports apply tremendously high rates. And it's not only the fees, but it's asking you to deposit a series of tremendously high bonds -- I would dare say not only high, but abusive -- to carry out these kinds of events. I insist that we talk about neighborhood associations, party commissions, non-profit entities totally. We have therefore found ourselves faced with this collection of taxes and the demand for bail by Ports, many of these entities are in a position to give up organizing activities that have been doing for many years because of the impossibility of dealing with these payments. From BNG we argue that in sports, cultural and leisure activities carried out in public spaces, neighborhoods must be involved, and their organization must not be left to professional operators. So expelling the neighborhood of these spaces is not an option that we defend in the BNG, nor are they defending a very important part of our neighbours and neighbours, with which we find ourselves even more of a question that we believe must be resolved, and that's why this amendment of the port law that today presents the BNG here. Next I'm going to make a tax relationship that's paying different municipalities for activities that have nothing to do with port activity. I shall begin, for example, by the council of Cambados — my council. These rates are not related to port activity, for example, the realization of the weekly market — which takes place twice a week, Wednesdays and Saturdays —, so it pays Galician ports a monthly amount of more than 3,000 euros. That is to say, annually he pays the municipality, for occupying an explanada of title of Ports of Galicia, an amount of more than 50,000 euros. A space that takes care of urbanizing, cleaning and conditioning the municipality itself, but that which is raised is Galician ports, an amount of more than 50,000 euros. By occupying the space called attached to the Town Hall, the Cambados Shoe, which at no point has absolutely no kind of port use, pays more than 15,000 euros. I mean, we're talking about the City of Cambados flying to Galician ports annually very close to 100,000 euros to carry out playful activities for all neighbors and neighbors in spaces where Galician ports don't make any port use at all. We are talking about the Council of Cambados, which has unanimously approved plenary agreements for more than ten years, asking the ports of Galicia to decommission these lands so that they become municipal ownership and that these situations become normal. We can talk about the Sanxenxo Town Hall, which for a space located in the Port of Portonovo Harbour Hall, for one week's parties, pays 6,000 euros. By putting on a tent to make the palliate in Portonovo a weekend, it pays about 3,500 euros. By putting a tent in the hall, one weekend, he pays another 1,500 euros. We can talk about the Grove Town Hall, the intention of the Galician Ports, to celebrate the Carme Party, to charge you 15,000 euros. And figures are also very important for celebrating the little market. We can talk about the Rianxo Town Hall, where Galician Ports came from charging 3,800 euros for the Guadalupe party in 2015, to charge 6,800 euros in 2017. We can talk about the Vilanova Town Hall, where the public school Julius Camba is on land in the ports of Galicia and is paid canon to Costas. Just like the Grove Town Hall. The Grove Town Hall itself is located on the grounds of Ports of Galicia, who pays a cannon for this situation. We can talk about the Fistra Town Hall, where 200 euros a day are paid for by the celebration of local parties. Or 1,000 euros for five days of Carme's parties. Or where abusive charges are paid around 1,200 euros per year on a terrace. We can talk about the Cangas Town Hall, where more than €10,000 is paid for the Carme parties, or more than €1.600 for the market. We can talk about Pontedeme Town Hall, where it pays for annual parties. Where it is paid for the performance of the Sport Party, where it is paid for the walk, the football field... From then on, in a normal relationship where there was a differentiation, in which there was a normal demarcation between urban spaces, of normal use of neighborhood, and the Ports' spaces, of normal use of port infrastructures, this situation would have to be more than solved, so there is a problem, there is an important discomfort in the municipalities of all political colors — of course, of all political colors —, municipalities that demand resolution and non-profit entities that are causing serious prejudice and that are giving up the realization of activities for the entire neighborhood on this issue. So we believe that this amendment to the Ports Act on this particular issue that we bring here today, the Nationalist Block of Galicia, is of course fair to take place. We propose to exempt the municipalities and non-profit entities from paying the fees for carrying out these recreational and sporting activities in public spaces; to initiate the procedure for the axial decommissionation of these spaces demanded by the municipalities that are in the urban plot and that do not have port uses. We propose the creation of a coordinating body between the Towns and Ports to normalize the relationship between Ports and the Towns, and not this current tax relationship by Ports, where there is no dialogue, but order and command; and the elimination of the sanctioning regime for bathing and fishing in port areas without prior authorization. We think it's a necessary change. The People's Party...

In any case, it is a law that makes it difficult to obtain authorizations for sporting and cultural events in the municipalities concerned, which have asked for the decommissioning of the harbour spaces that currently do not have these uses, and in which the pecuniary demands imposed by the public entity Ports of Galicia to allow events to be held in the lands of their competition endanger the celebration of holidays in different municipalities, as they have already reported in Salnès. A legislation that leads to absolute neglect and allegations of lack of maintenance and conservation of spaces that do not have port use. The only reason to legally maintain this anachronistic situation in a struggle for collecting power, is, in our group's view, the benefits of disproportionate tolls imposed on the municipalities for the normal development of their citizens' activities. A recollective and mercantilist vision of Galician ports in which the figures are disproportionate to the budgets that manage small municipalities like Fisterra, where he forced to pay more than 12,000 euros for the installation of terraces and bars, the 24,000 that paid O Grove for the Marish Festival, and fines up to 60,000 euros for fishing in port areas. These are the problems that lead to many local entities that a part of their urban core is held by Ports de Galicia. So we are faced with a situation that is not adapted to the new social and urban reality of the municipalities and must be changed. For everyone it is known that, over the past few decades, certain works have been carried out that have made the villages grow, but possibly what years ago had a sense that was not of port ownership, now it doesn't. societies and villages change, and regulations must also do so, but unfortunately the Galician Union has remained in the past, particularly before 1975. To conclude, the concrete amendments to Law 6/2017, of 12 December, of the Ports of Galicia, would be the creation of a municipal port coordination body responsible for articulating the relations between the port administrations and the municipal administrations, and on which the specific conditions would be laid down in that body's head, both in its composition and in its competences. It is absolutely necessary to establish mechanisms that prevent different views from being applied on the same territory and, in many cases, contrasting when they occupy the same spaces and must satisfy the same citizenship. In many cases, ports are used as a tool of partisan attack when the color of the municipality and port authority is different, something unacceptable in terms of economic and port development. The exclusion of sport fishing and the prohibition on the development of marine and fishing activities in the inland waters of the port, constituting zone 1 where the limits and their definition are to be established. The exemption of local and non-profit entities from the contents of Section 2a of Chapter 2 of Title III, so that permits for leisure and similar facilities, proposed by local authorities, do not have to follow this procedure. Stop considering a breach of bathing and fishing activities, and add a new provision to establish that urban spaces, which, even belonging to Galician ports, are fully integrated into the municipal urban plot, are transferred to the municipalities if so requested. They therefore seem to us to be all appropriate measures for the benefit of the citizenship of the coastal municipalities currently affected by that law, which the Union should immediately change if what it wants is to legislate for the Greeks and the Greeks instead of benefiting from a right from the past. Nothing else. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-ES-GA_2018-12-04-DSPG094.u18	Thank you, Mrs. Prado. As already reported to the groups, the proposed law of the authorship of the Parliamentary Group of En Marea was approved. So now we continue with the other bill, which appears in third place. Debate taken into account by the G.P. of the Socialists of Galicia, for the promotion of social responsibility in Galicia
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2018-12-04-DSPG094.u19	Mrs. Rodríguez Rumbo has the floor to present the bill.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2018-12-04-DSPG094.u20	Thank you, Mr. President. Good morning, ladies and gentlemen. The Socialists today bring a bill on social responsibility. Social responsibility — as you all know —, ladies and gentlemen, is a commitment to citizenship. Social responsibility must be understood as the way to address public policies based on the management of the impacts that these public policies generate on citizenship and on the environment. You know that social responsibility began within the European Union in 2001, with the presentation of the Green Paper on corporate social responsibility. Seventeen years later, we understand that there is no legal framework in which this commitment of the Greek community to social responsibility is placed. So we socialists today bring this bill for its consideration, waiting for a positive welcome from all political groups. Because citizenship, gentlemen, would not understand that the Galician Union does not lead a project to advance towards the Europe 2020 Strategy, geared to smart, sustainable and inclusive growth. Because citizenship would not understand that the Galician Union does not develop socially responsible actions and policies within the framework of the autonomous administration. But I would also not understand the citizenship that the Galician Union is not a vertebrate element, of guidance and support for local entities, for businesses, entities and organizations, self-employed people and therefore citizenship as a whole, so that they can put in place policies, plans, programmes or projects in which social, environmental and economic sustainability criteria are assumed. Social responsibility is therefore the commitment to voluntarily adopt measures to achieve a more just and environmentally sustainable society. And this commitment requires a legal framework that regulates initiatives within a strategy of its own. The social responsibility contained in this bill is based on four fundamental pillars, obviously starting — as it cannot be otherwise — from strict compliance with the legal minimums established. The pillars are employment, to avoid job precariousness; social, to seek social integration; the environment, to take care of the environment; and good government practices. Social responsibility for employment presents measures necessary for the promotion of quality in employment: continuous improvements in labour and remuneration conditions, stable employment creation and reduction of temporality or interimity, continuing training, vocational training, improvement in employment conditions, taking into account the premises of social dialogue, collective bargaining and participation, promoting innovation and entrepreneurship, promoting self-employment and the social economy. Social responsibility in the social field sets out the necessary measures to promote the inclusion of groups at risk of social exclusion, equal treatment and non-discrimination, equal opportunities between women and men, equal pay, reconciliation of personal, labour and family life and the full accessibility of people with functional diversity. [[Applause]] Social responsibility in the environment proposes measures to minimize environmental impact and carbon footprint, reduce the effects of climate change, responsible and sustainable consumption, promote biodiversity and the preservation of the Greek ecosystems, landscape and cultural and historical heritage. Finally, the fourth pillar, in good government, takes the necessary measures to have regard to the development of ethical codes; transparency, management and access to public information; improvement of the quality of services provided to citizenship; reduction of administrative burdens; and socially responsible public procurement — point where I will stop later —. This bill consists of 29 articles, 3 additional provisions, 1 derogation provision and 1 last provision. Title I of this law contains, on the one hand, the adoption of a Greek social responsibility plan for Galicia, as a planning and management tool to carry out its policy in coordination with the state plans on the subject. The objectives, measures, actions and indicators to be adopted during their lifetime will be set in this plan. An annual memory is also established, including the set of policies, measures and actions developed by the Galician Union and its instrumental public sector, which are within the scope of social responsibility. Title II speaks of social responsibility in the Greek entities, and collects as socially responsible entities workers and self-employed persons, companies and entities and all kinds of public and private organizations who, qualified in accordance with the procedure provided for in this law or in their regulatory development, have adopted voluntary integration into their government, in their management and in their business strategy of the values of social responsibility set out in this bill. It also includes the evaluation of social responsibility, which will be through a memory that collects analytical results, as well as the evaluation of aspects that integrate social responsibility. It also includes verification of social responsibility through external audits; the badge that believes these Galician entities as socially responsible; a register of socially responsible Galician entities and a number of benefits that would be granted from the declaration of these socially responsible Galician entities. Benefits that may be in the field of public procurement, in the matter of granting public aid and subsidies or reputational benefits, such as exhibiting the badge or being able to use it in all its advertising as socially responsible companies. Certain tax and publicity benefits can be established to identify companies qualified as socially responsible entities in Galicia. Social and business responsibility awards are also foreseen in Galicia, which would be annual, to reward those entities who, previously qualified as socially responsible entities, highlighted in the promotion of these values of social responsibility. And finally, in Title III, the Galego Social Responsibility Council, which would be the reference body to promote and advise the programming of the public policies of the Autonomous Administration in the field of social responsibility. The actions to be taken by the Galician Union for the promotion of social responsibility are also envisaged, such as subsidising the performance of certificates of standards or standards of social responsibility; subsidising the development of equality plans; encouraging the achievement of the Greek brand of excellence on an equal footing; encouraging work conciliation measures or promoting advisory programmes for entities. So we understand that social responsibility is an issue that can't be postponed for longer, that has to be promoted from now on and that the Galician Union must be that driving thread so that social responsibility spreads to the whole social layer, to the whole citizenship and to all the Greek companies, little by little, and with its help. [[Applause]] As I said at the outset, this proposal for a law of social responsibility has a fundamental aspect in what I'm going to stop a little, which speaks of socially responsible public procurement. The Autonomic Administration must use public procurement as a strategic tool to incorporate social, environmental and ethical criteria into recruitment requirements. The Autonomic Administration must therefore promote socially responsible public procurement, promote integrity pacts in public procurement and also reserve participation in the adjudication processes of certain labour contracts, supplies and services to special employment centres and insertion companies. The Autonomic Administration must include social liability clauses in public procurement in a variety of ways. Incorporating a contract clause in all public contracts establishing that persons or entities, contractors or subcontractors, subsidiary undertakings or interposts, cannot carry out financial operations in tax havens that are considered to be criminal, avoiding money laundering, tax fraud or offences against the public farm. You have to incorporate the warning that the contract is subject to compliance with the current legal provisions that result in employment, social security or health at work. It must incorporate the obligation of tendering persons or entities to indicate the collective agreement establishing the most beneficial conditions which shall be compulsory for persons carrying out the activity subject to the contract. The person or adjudicator shall, throughout the life of the contract, pay the salary collected in the collective application agreement establishing those most beneficial conditions. And in those contracts requiring the adjudicate to be obliged to subrogate as an employer in certain labour relations, you should inform the tenderers of the conditions of the contracts of workers and/or workers affecting the subrogation that is necessary in order to be able to assess the labour costs involved in such a measure. They must also determine the criteria for adjudicating the contract to the most advantageous offer, but not only should the lowest price be taken into account, but also efficiency parameters, as long as they do not affect the impairment of labour, wage and social conditions of workers and workers affected by recruitment or subcontracting. It shall also include environmentally friendly products as a criterion for the adjudication or condition of the execution of a contract. Finally, it will include non-discriminatory language clauses relating to the use of Greek during the execution of contracts. Therefore, ladies and gentlemen of all political groups, today we have the opportunity to vote in favour of the consideration of this bill, so that it will have a result that will have an impact on the improvement of our entire citizenship, as I said at the outset, by making a more just and environmentally sustainable management. Nothing else in this first intervention. Thank you very much.

The opinion of Mrs. Sea advisor, on his own request, to account for the line of work towards the negotiation of total allowable catches and quotas for 2019
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2018-12-04-DSPG094.u41	The sea councillor, Mrs. Rosa Quintana Carballo, has the floor.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2018-12-04-DSPG094.u42	Thank you, Mr. President. Ladies and gentlemen, very good morning. I share today in this House to take account of Galicia's position and proposal on the allocation of TACs and quotas which will take place at the next Council of Fisheries Ministers of the European Union, and also to take account of the work that we have worked on in this community to put our arguments in place before Brussels. Galicia and its fishing sector have a lot to do with this guest. In this debate we will have to combine the need to achieve the most sustainable yield, knowing that only two years are left to do so, as well as to insert the most complex part of the landing obligation. Galicia joins this council with the responsibility to bring with it the representation of autonomous communities within the delegation of the Kingdom of Spain. For this, we are closing a document with the aim of setting up a common demand position where data is being finalized and inserting aspects such as the recent agreement in the NEAF core. That common position must have a fundamental piar, supported in work with the sector for the benefit of our fishing, our shellfish and our aquaculture: co-governance. This co-governance was addressed within the Galego Fisheries Council, and within it we reached a unanimous agreement on the Commission's communication on the situation of the common fisheries policy and the consultation on fishing opportunities for 2019; agreements which were taken by the Consultative Council for Community Affairs in Madrid with the aim of taking account of Spain's position in negotiations with Brussels itself. This communication addresses the basic issues of the common fisheries policy, issues which are relevant to the work we have carried out in order to adapt this application of the common fisheries policy to our circumstances and, in particular, in two aspects: multiannual plans and discards. With regard to multiannual plans, Galicia understands and agrees with the need to have these tools available through the application of ranges or mortality intervals that allow greater flexibility to be offered when fixing TACs. As far as the challenge of current fishing is concerned, the decline in discards is concerned, Galicia has done its duty to account for them and value them. This is both in the potentially affected fleets and in those who can see their work diminished, even if it is scarce. In any case, Galicia has come up with proposals that allow it to continue working on the fleet while it can move forward towards that goal of minimizing and eliminating discards. That was the mandate of the Galician Fisheries Council in its ruling on reform of the common fisheries policy, and so we will defend it. The proposal for a TAC and quotas regulation which the European Commission has made a little less than a month ago has taken up all or part of some of the demands made. As far as regulation and general aspects are concerned, we would have three aspects to focus on: sustainable maximum yield, discards and fisheries management. Within the maximum sustainable yield, and after pointing at our demand for flexibility to reach this level when there may be serious social or economic impact, we have a fundamental aspect that can undergo a substantial change in this area. We're talking about determining TACs in some cases, where feasible, based on the recommendation of the top level of the RMS range as a formula for better raising available quotas. We have as an example the case of Cantabrian fish, where its application could increase the TAC by 21%. As for this, the plan will have to come into force, and this is planned for March, we consider it important to raise the demand for the Council to meet the request for the revision of those TACs which may be improved without affecting the state of biomass, always at those intervals and adapting that higher level of recommendation. In relation to discards, our analysis and proposal focuses on three important points. The first, which follows the Commission's proposal, is to create a common TAC for by-catches and what we generally agree on. In this sense, it must be borne in mind that this Commission proposal is afraid of many Member States, which are responding to the need to apply to them the stability of these TACs for accessory fishing. Therefore, in this respect we do not agree with the kindness applied in the case of TACs attributed to species with recommendation of TAC zero, which are for five species in which we do not have quota, but we see that the amounts granted are extremely important, arguing for this fact the possibility that they become strangulation species. The second point has to do with the demand to eliminate those TACs of species of accessory capture for our fleet and that can become, these yes, into species of strangulation. These nine TACs have in common that there is no fishing effort directed at them, which has significantly reduced the fishing effort on these species, which have a low-considered landing volume, and that the landed quantities are stable and adapted to TACs. In addition, there are precedents for the elimination of TACs based on this risk and according to these criteria, such as berthella or beach in certain areas. In this case, our proposal will be addressed to the abbot, the linguado, the stick fish, the melon and the sole. The third important issue is the allocation of the fishing quota, but addressed to those species for which we have never had fishing opportunities and which do not appear in the Commission's proposal. In the face of the possibility that they end up becoming species of strangulation, we demand a quota accessory of ochavo, or clock, and of eglefin, or donkey, that allows them to work on our ships without having to stop because of their indisposition. With regard to fisheries management and the determination of TACs between areas, from Galicia we want to point out and demand improvements that can lead to progress in the use and development of activity by the sector. The analysis and proposal document ends up in three blocks of aspects related to fisheries management. These are: The first one has to do with the differentiation of management or allocation of fishing potential figures for the same species in different TAC areas. We're talking about the case of the lire and the garden, where we understand that a different management could be done from the northwestern Cantabrian area and assign a higher quota due to the different fishing practice that is done in it with respect to the waters of the rest of the North Atlantic. We understand that this different management can be based on the different orientation of fishing, being in our case for direct human consumption and the small impact of our catches on the general TAC of NEAF, since we represent only 4.2 % of the lire and 3.7 % of the yard. In addition, we have as an example the different proposal put forward for the North Sea on the basis of the fishing area, since, starting from a single ICES report, we see how the North Sea proposes a 37% increase, the Great Sun 28% and the French area 23%. This situation, clearly unexplained and unexplained, brings another unconsumed attempt from the TAC of Rape in waters of Scotland to the North Sea, recasting the request between both areas and in which Galicia would lose in its interests not to mediate the intervention before Spain and Europe. The second aspect to consider has to do with the possible separation or overlap of different TACs of pelagic species, such as the jurel or the mouth. In this sense, we advocate secondary to the unexplained position of ICES by separating the TACs from the width of the south and north of the waters of zone 9, between Fisterra and Gibraltar, maximum when in our area we have less quota and it seems that it is the best state to present. Moreover, we argue again that it is possible to extend to 25% the flexibility of exchange of interest and width between the Cantabria and Fisterra areas to the south, which is now around 5%. The third of the important aspects raises several problems in relation to management, the most important being that of the substantial variation in quotas between annualities. We agree with a proposal that quota reductions or enlargements do not exceed 15%, making a smoother approach to the levels that must be reached, so this is the most assumed point for our fleet. The second case has to do with the necessary clarification of the validity of the EEC Regulation 2.166 of the year 2005 for the recovery of fish and cicada in Cantabrico and Northwest, and which does not apply in the case of the most favourable determination of the TAC but in the limitation of fishing days, although they increase to the year 2019. In addition, we want to pay special attention to track management so that TAC determinations are further segregated to those varieties that are in the worst state, making the general figure unconditional to them. Ladies and gentlemen, all these general aspects have a practical and specific application, which reflects the allocation of quotas within each of the TACs stipulated for each species and according to the areas determined by ICES. With this proposal we have on the table this year, we can say that this is not the worst exit position we've ever found. We might well say, even, that it is one of the most advantageous, although much remains to be negotiated and that we will come up with all our arguments to try to improve the proposal. Of the 36 main TACs that Spain has quota, we have to take into account that for 14 we are proposing an increase; in 10 we would keep the TACs for 2018, and in 10 we propose a discount, among which we count the lire and the jarda, already known but officially unreported. They'd only be missing to know the two proposals corresponding to the width, or the mouth, that go from June to June and not from January to January. In this sense, pending the possible separation of the TAC from the south and taking into account the good results collected in the Cantabrian campaign, it seems that in this species we could have good news. However, it is necessary to clarify the situation of the TAC proposal for the Fisterra wide towards the south. It appears in the texts with a zero figure, which we don't like in any of the States that share the TAC — Spain and Portugal — or Galicia, so we demand that this figure be changed even knowing that it is temporary and that it does not reflect the final result. This year we would have been among the positive points already known to several TACs of traditional importance for Galicia, in addition to other quotas of species of accessory capture that become important in the implementation of the obligation to land. In this sense, we have to know that among these positive points is the 27% rise in the Great Sun and 23% in the waters of France, making this species refer to its good state and can be consolidated as the largest invoicing for Galicia; the increase in rapping quota by 1% in the Cantabrian, and the rise of that species in waters of Scotland -- where we have several British flag vessels dedicated to this fishing -- and the increase in the TAC of the jurelo, both in waters of Fiji to the south, where it rises 69%, as well as in the waters of the Cantabrian, where it grows by 18%. The rapist also sees his figures rise, going from 47% to the Great Sun, going from 35% to 40% for the Cantabria, to 40% for the French waters, and 6% for the Skopjes. They also raise the cicada's quotas, both in Scotland's waters, at 24%, as well as in the waters of Fistra towards the south, at 5%, and in the French waters of the Gulf of Biscay, where the increase reaches 7%. Finally, the tenth-quarter growing TAC is that of the fluctuated in the waters of the Gulf of Biscay, where it would have an addition of 5% over the year 2018. For its part, the Commission's proposal points to a possible continuity of TACs which would host the following species: The abbexus, who would see an unchanged proposal for the five areas where Spain has quota, both in the Great Sun and in the Cantabrian and northwest waters. The language in the Cantabria, an important accessory species that sees its stability in the quota we've dragged since 2011. The liba, or merlan, in the Cantabrian, which also has the same number of TACs since 2016, and which is presented as an accessory fishing. The shellfish in Gran Sol and Hatton Bank, whose biomass stability is reflected in this continuity of TAC and which is a secondary fishing for many of the Greek ships. The strips in the Cantabria, which assume a complementary species for our fleet, mainly for the craftsmanship, and which may account for an important income accompanying the hundredth fishing vessel. Galicia also wants to record those negative points that we see in the proposal that can affect our fleet. In this case of negativity, there are 10 proposals for TACs of different impact and impact, but we will try to reverse or diminish. In this supposing are the Cantabrian and Northwest fish, with a 14% reduction request, but which has several possibilities between ICES considerations to improve their TAC number. In this sense, a determination of TAC according to the higher level of the sustainable maximum yield range could bring 21% more fishing opportunities and even improve biomass. In this same assumption, it would also be the alternative to considering a proposal according to the recovery plan, whose vigilance is unclear and which would make it possible to add up to 15% more increase in both biomass and fishing opportunities. Finally, if the TAC were to be maintained, biomass would improve by 45%, an important issue that can be achieved in a broader but always rigorous time frame if we take other alternatives. Another point of inconformity would be rooted in the continuity of the effective closure of the cicada in Cantabrico, since the study campaigns carried out by the Spanish Institute of Oceanography, with the collaboration of the sector, offer more positive figures than those brought up by the ICES for closure, so we demand its reconsideration. Also, speaking of the cock, we have observed with concern the proposal to reduce the TAC of this species to the Great Sun, encrypted in a 31% adjustment, if well in the unit where our fishing fleet generally this species is cut by 3%. We've still seen this proposal with great concern. For the rappel, there is a decrease in quotas in waters of the Great Sun by 1%, and also in waters of France by 6%. But ICES reports seem to grant a better state of stock, so we hope that the Commission will reconsider this proposal and even improve it. In this same situation, there is also the Great Sun's TAC, where the proposed figure is adjusted by 1%. However, we expect that state to reflect ICES reports to lead to continuity, and even a possible rise in the quota. Consideration of the part deserves the proposal to lower the fish in waters west of Hatton Bank, since a cut of 20% does not seem to coincide with the recommendations of the ICES, which calls for a closure throughout the northern area. Against all criteria, and yet there is a reduction of effort on our part in those waters and not consuming the TAC, this cut contrasts with the continuity of those existing in the North Sea into Danish waters, where the TAC is overcome. We hope that this discrepancy and apparent inconsistencies can be explained and corrected, because if it does not have a high economic impact, it can be a case of kind of strangulation. As I said, ladies and gentlemen, we are working to consolidate the figures of each of these TACs that grow to reverse or diminish the download in other cases. For this purpose, we are reviewing species by species, TAC by TAC, and analyzing several sources of information to verify and conform our arguments. We are evaluating the ICES reports to be realistic in requests and not going beyond the sustainable, we are evaluating the landings of each species and of each fleet to see the level of involvement of each download, we are evaluating the comments of other countries in the working groups of the European Union to seek allies, we are analyzing the trend of TACs in other years to see their evolution and we are assessing the importance of the economic impact of each species to center demands and establish a prevalence in negotiation. Chapter separate deserves the TACs assigned to the lire and the yard, and whose determination is made in the bosom of NEAF, this entity that unites all the coastal countries of the northwest Atlantic. And we have to say that in this sense the agreements reached over the past few weeks seem to set an adjustment of 20% on both species, although this proposal has not yet been officially communicated. In this respect it is true that the ICES report for the yard did not call for good forecasts, but the opinion of the Cantabrian sector does not seem to agree with this vision of the ICES, so we demand that, since there is no differentiated report — that should be — if you can make a differentiated allocation. In this very case, it would be the lire. And we would like to reiterate here that these bad scientific prospects should not be attributed to Spain, because its volume of fishing vessels, as I told you at first, is neither substantial nor decisive in the state of stocks that cause this fall in TACs. That is why we also want to ask for an explanation and correction in those deductions which the Commission intends to carry out in some TACs and which would require a correction between catches and landings, although the figures are not homogeneous between areas despite having very similar patterns of exploitation. This case is particularly sensitive in the case of fish, rapist and rapist, since a deduction is assigned in the chaos of France's waters and Cantabrian and Northwest calator that does not replicate and reproduce in the case of other waters of the north, such as zones 4, 6 and 7. In addition, this proposal for a regulation contains a list of measures which affect only two species which today are not subject to TAC and quotas but which are limited to their fishing operation by the regulations which collect this document. I am talking about the case of anguy, in which the Commission intends to extend the capture of the angle to three consecutive months, to be available between September and January; a question which seems consistent, since before the juveniles of this species were not protected, and whether the adult copies. The other case in this respect would be that of looting, where we should be careful that the important restrictions that are being imposed on northern waters do not extend to those of Cantabrian and Northwest, where our capture is highly selective and sustainable. In addition, this attention must be redoubled, since the multiannual plan for western waters includes this species among those managed, which could well be the prelude to the introduction of a TAC, which has been denounced by Spain and other Member States and denied by the Commission. In addition, and not yet included in this proposal, we must not lose sight of the Sardinian situation, whose adjustments through the joint plan between Spain and Portugal have been approved by the Commission. In this sense, we hope that they will be effective in changing the descending line of the species and dissipating the possibilities for the imposition of a TAC by Europe, which would, surely, be more stringent. All of this contains our analysis and our demand, all of this will be communicated to the Ministry so that, in a coordinated way and by bringing up all our arguments, we can jointly reverse these situations that I have just pointed out. The goal is only to achieve the best situation for our fleet in a year that can be a key to your future. Because, ladies and gentlemen, speaking of a key year, we must not forget that Brexit is planning this deal. A country that cannot assume as normal its deindustrialization, as if it were something inexorable. It's been over a month and a half since Alcoa announced Alcoa's closure in Avilés and also in the Coruña, and since then we have to regret that there have been more words than directly made by responsible administrations. And we have to say that we are not satisfied with the role that these played, neither with the role of the State, nor - we have to say - with the role of the Galician Union and with its degree of compliance, or rather failure, with the agreements we have reached in this chamber. And, of course, from our point of view, this is not the way to find a solution to such an important problem from the point of view of the country. Because today we're talking about Alcoa, we're talking about over 400 jobs, over 400 families, but by extension we're also talking about the rest of our country's electro-intensive industry and whether we're going to give a stable framework for them to go ahead or whether we're going to move forward in our country's deindustrialization. We understand that the response to this situation has two major pillars, and this motion seeks to address both legs: on the one hand, the urgent measures that need the situation of Alcohol and, on the other hand, also the changes that need the energy policy to shield the future of our country's industry. I will therefore focus first on Alcoa's emergencies. On October 24, you will remember your lordships, a week after the company announced its intention to close, we thought that this parliament really, at an early stage, was up, we all reached an agreement in this chamber to the thread of an institutional declaration that seemed to us a good starting point. Since the opposition, all political forces have yielded and come to an agreement. But we have to say that we are disappointed in how this has been accomplished or rather — I insist — failing. We say it out of honesty. Then someone from the People's Party will come and accuse us of being politicized and so on; a little bit on the line -- I hope not so sad -- as the industrial adviser had in the interrogation and in the questions that my organization posed. But the truth is, in honesty we have to say that to us it is so. However, we are prepared and we think it's important to maintain a unitary position. But also that call to unity can't be the disguise so as not to say what's going wrong and not to complain also that there's little transparency, little consensus, but above all few results. And our political responsibility leads us to formulate issues that we believe are unappealable in this motion. The balance of the Galician Union so far is not satisfactory to us, because we think it failed to comply with the unit of action, it did not take into account at any time the arrivals of parliamentary groups, and today I believe that all of us can hear on our public radio as employees of this company say that the news that comes to them are by the press and not by the mouth of the institutions. It seems to us that this one, of course, is not the way. And we understand that the Galician Union should, firstly, commit itself to greater compliance with the agreements of both this 24 October in this Parliament and with the issues which the company committee itself made on 30 October. In particular, a question that we don't understand because it's not yet a reality, which is a work table where these workers can have a direct voice. We understand, therefore, that for the first point of our motion there is no single argument to vote against. As for the second point, what we are doing is to introduce a question which we understand cannot be further debated: that the sooner we find a consensus on that question more prepared and prepared we will be in favour of whether we have to resort to it. And we bet directly because if we value from the central government the possibility of a public intervention in Alcoa, something as simple as that. We don't understand why there are still vetoes around this issue; it's not something exceptional, it's not something that doesn't consider the legal order and it's not something that has not been done in the Spanish State itself or in the European environment itself in similar situations. So we introduced in our motion a specific point, point 2, to bet on public intervention in Alcoa, either to wait for a buyer or to return it to the public domain of what perhaps, if it hadn't come out thanks to Mr Aznar, because we wouldn't be in this situation. But I was saying at the beginning of my intervention that we thought not only about the urgency of Alcoa, but we also have to see that there is beyond the urgency, and try not to comply with what appears in the famous Mafalda cartoon, where it is said that "the urgent doesn't leave time for the important one." We think that the stage that opens, the debates that are being opened must also serve Galicia to put itself on this fundamental issue. Because really what we're seeing today in Alcoa is the sneezing failure of an energy policy fundamentally emanating from the central government, but also from the Galician Union with its silence and absence, which has given an alibi to the electro-intensive industries not to modernize themselves and which has led us to an absolutely unstable and damaging framework. By setting an example, today companies like Alcoa, like Megasa or Ferroatlantic don't know how much megawatt will pay next January 1st. It's less than a month left and you don't know how much the price of electricity consumption will be for your own finances! This is absolutely delusional. How it's disappointing that all of this depends on a 20-second pox that's going to be held next week. We've insisted on this over and over again on legislation, and we're doing it again today with a proposal that bets on a stable tariff system. Something that is perfectly possible, because it has already existed in the Spanish State and exists in other countries of the European environment. It's also a demand from the sector. We understand that there is another issue that is fundamental, and connected to this we know that next week there will be a pause of interruption. We know that we have been moving in this system for a year and a half, and we — we insist — have brought this proposal to parliament several times, that there is a table where this is evaluated. Recently, the Galician Union finally called for a table with the sector, but we understand that a fundamental foot is still missing: we cannot move forward on that table without the social part. That's why we asked point 3 for its incorporation. And this must also be linked to making proposals and moving forward towards that fundamental issue that concerns us so much, which is another industrial policy, because it is worrying about the absence of industrial policy. And in the case of Alcoa it's paradigmatic how for many years this company received millions and millions of the public era and nobody checked that it was doing the necessary modernization. We understand that we have to turn it around, we have to change the current system of interruption for an industrial tariff system that is stable in time, which has predictable variations for electrointensive power supply, but not in return for nothing. We understand that there are three conditions that have to go from the public domain that must be vigilant: first of all, the obligation to make investments to modernise their productive structure, also the obligation to maintain jobs and, of course, through an industrial development plan that must be monitored from the public authorities. ParlaMint-ES-GA_2018-12-04-DSPG094.u179	To formulate the motion, Mrs. Quinteiro has the floor.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2018-12-04-DSPG094.u180	Thank you, President. Good afternoon. Today we are debating a motion as a result of the interpellation we brought in the past full, referring to the workers who clean hotels and hostels — the Kellys. The first thing we have to say is that, at least as far as attitudes are concerned, we believe that the PP has changed a little attitude from the past full to this, that at least amend our motion, and we think that there will be a deal. Because the truth is, we cannot help but mark the inappropriate statement of the counsellor's statements in the previous plenary. We believe that he showed a total lack of empathy and we have to point out that it can't be that in the face of such a obvious reality, that they put the workers themselves on the table — because they were present right here — if they ignore this reality without any kind of potion. It seems totally unacceptable to us that an Employment Counsellor had made the statements he made in the previous plenary. As we have already stated on other occasions, we are talking about a strongly precarious, heavily feminized sector, which has also been seeing a long time since its working conditions have been getting worse progressively, and that the only thing they are demanding are actions decided by the Administration. They demand minimum working conditions worthy of being able to live a life with a minimum dignity. Because we understand that it is not acceptable for a sector like this, so precarious, with situations so difficult to reach often even at the end of the month, in the face of such an unfair situation, there is practically nothing being done. We believe that it is necessary to put an end to the precarious work of these women once and for all. We know — they themselves tell us, and they are visualizing and doing very important work to visualize this situation — that they have endless work days, they have many times hours of restless work, they are often charging for misery — even less than two and a half euros per room —, often have professional diseases derived from the excessive burden of work, they have to endure many totally inappropriate comments in the mutuals — as we have already noted in the past debate, it is true that progress has been made and that at last some of the professional diseases they have suffered have been recognized — and — as I said — they have been seeing too long as this situation is going to worse every day, as every day the situation is more precarious, without any administration taking letters in the matter. That's why we bring this motion today, because we believe that we can't wait any longer, and we hope that from here today comes a first step towards visibility, recognition and for all the measures needed to not live this precarious reality. We believe it's not acceptable that there are so many people, especially so many women, who work and can't reach the end of the month, so many people who work and are poor. That's a reality that's already too wide in our country: more and more people have poor wages, temporary jobs, unstable jobs, and we believe it's totally intolerable. Today we are talking about a sector, a sector that is especially hit not only by the crisis, but is especially hit and invisible for a long time. But it's a reality that's already too broad and -- as my partner often says -- it's already a structural feature of the labour market. And before we come in to talk about the proposal that we bring today and to talk in detail about the points that we have presented, we wanted to send from here all our solidarity and strength to women who are being retaliation and who are being unfairly fired for mobilizing themselves. Today we want to remember especially from a woman who was unjustly fired from the Eurostars Gran Hotel in Compostela, and who tomorrow will mobilize together with many other companions and with many other people who were fired only by mobilising themselves and claiming dignified conditions. [[Applause]] As I say, unfortunately, this is one more example; we have already commented on it: many women report coercion, many women report changes in their job for worse conditions when they require a minimum improvement, many of them do not renew the temporary contract after they appear in a concentration or in a mobilization, many women, unfortunately, cannot again work in that prison when the only one they demand is a minimum wage. We believe that, as I said, the situation is totally unsustainable. Our motion has several paragraphs. On the one hand, we believe that one of the first things to do in this sector — and in general — is to listen to and know the first-hand sector. We believe that it was clear from the counsellor's intervention that the Council of Galicia deeply does not know what is happening in this sector; or, well, it does not know it without any kind of smell. We want to think that you don't know, and that's why our first point is to seek and deepen in the studies on occupational health and occupational safety to thoroughly assess the conditions that are being given at this time in the work of hotels and hostels. We believe it is not acceptable for women — and many witnesses tell us — to work with analgesics. We think it's not an isolated case; it's a situation that's already too general, that happens many times, and we think that we also have to evaluate why this is happening and do a deep study. On the other hand, as I said, we believe it is important to listen to the sector and first-hand to know what their demands are and what their situation is. So one point is to promote social dialogue, because we believe it can't be that employers continue without listening; they have to sit down and listen to trade union organizations. You have to say that it's enough, that it can't be that they keep getting rich at the expense of exploiting workers more and more. That's why we believe that these two points are a first basis for getting to know the situation and improving it. On the other hand, we believe that public aid cannot be an empty check. As we have also said in many other sectors, we deny that the money that is from all of them goes to certain companies that operate, to certain companies that have labour inspectorate records, which have crimes against the safety and health of workers. We believe that subsidies can never be a blank check again, we have to put some kind of control measure in order for public subsidies to go to companies that, at least, keep working conditions minimally dignified and do not exploit their workers. On the other hand, we also believe that it is important to visualize, because it is true that it is a reality that is coming out of light, but that it is still very invisible in many sectors of the population. That's why we believe the creation of a quality stamp for certain hotels helps to fight precariousness, because we know that many people prefer to go to a hotel that doesn't explode, that doesn't precarious. Because there are a lot of people who don't want to pay four hundred euros for a room and know that that worker is paying less than two and a half euros an hour — four hundred is a bit too much, but they don't want to pay 200 euros per room and know that there are workers with wages of misery. So we believe that the creation of this stamp, which has not been launched but has already been approved in other parts of the country, can be positive to see how the situation of the different shelters is. On the other hand, we believe it is essential to move forward on the subject of labour inspections. It's also a very recurring issue: it's said that there's not enough inspections, that it's often warned of inspections, that there's a lot of conditions that are completely illegal that are going on unnoticed... and that's why we believe that there's a need to increase labour inspections, and labour inspectors to demand the central government to do so. And from here, obviously, press for this to happen. Then -- as I said -- the infernal rhythms that these women live have many health problems and many occupational diseases, so we believe that reducing retirement age or studying the reasons for lowering retirement age is also a very necessary matter. And we believe that the central government must also listen to this claim, which is very basic, because there are many women who cannot continue to exercise their work because of this excessive workload. And lastly — which is the last point but not least — I would say the main one, because it goes to the root, because it talks about this sector but talks about all the sectors of workers and workers in our country — to ban labour reforms. Because we have to go to the root of the problem, because labor reforms have not managed to create more and better jobs — as many times have been said —; all they have achieved is to rob of rights, make up for wages and reduce layoffs. We believe that there must be an end to labour reforms; we know that there is no agreement at these points, but it seems to us essential that we review it and evaluate it. Years after the implementation of labour reforms, all those issues that were said to allow labour reforms, where are they? Where's all that high-quality job and job? Everything's the other way around. And, of course, labour reforms go hand in hand with the outsourcing of services, and — as we have said in many other sectors as well — outsourcing favours workers to have even worse wages, favours them to have much more instability, companies to have much more ease to fire the workers and to get them to go around constantly, and to get rid of the collective agreement. We believe that going to the root of the problem, ending these work reforms, is, let us say, the first step. As I say, this motion is a first step. It would probably take a lot more questions, it's probably not the only sector. Today we are talking about this sector because we believe it is a particularly vulnerable sector that is also visible and mobilizing and that has been silent for many years, but we believe that there are many precarious sectors in our country. ParlaMint-ES-GA_2018-12-04-DSPG094.u221	To formulate the non-law proposal, Mrs Arias has the floor.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2018-12-04-DSPG094.u222	Thank you, President. Ladies and gentlemen, good afternoon. This initiative speaks of the equality of all Welsh people, regardless of where they live, when they exercise their right to vote, and thus participate in the election of their political representatives with equal guarantees, with the same guarantees. Finally, it speaks of the fact that the Greeks who live abroad can exercise that right in an agile, safe and transparent manner, something which is reflected in the Constitution — which, by the way, we are celebrating this week's 40th anniversary — but also in the Statute for the Autonomy of Galicia. With this initiative, from the Popular Group, we ask that the Spaniards, who do not live in Spain, be able to approach this electoral process as much as possible to those who live in Galicia and those who live in Spain. And for that it is essential, firstly, to remove the voting system, to establish formulas so that residents abroad can vote simply, and to do so with the maximum guarantees, and to discard any option that implies doubts about the validity of votes and the cleaning of the whole process; that is, to totally discard back to the system prior to the reform of 2011. I'm not going to spend much time here explaining what the current situation is, since you all know it, but I'm going to make some points to contextualize this initiative. The reform of the organic law of general electoral regime, of 2011, driven by the Socialist Government of Zapatero, was born with the idea of eliminating fraud in the vote coming from the outside that was happening with the previous system and that was being denounced by all. So, it amends Article 75 of LOREX and it inserts the system of begging the vote, known as the voting system: a system that was more reliable, yes, but that had such complexity that it passes bill to our democratic system, and voting has since become, for those who reside outside Spain, a true career of obstacles. In Galicia, in the last beginnings of 2016, this system affected four hundred and forty-six thousand Greeks living outside Spain. In particular, the current system implies that those who live in the foreign country and want to participate in the autonomous elections, in the general elections or in the European elections must request the vote through the consulate, sending a form to the corresponding polling station — via mail, fax or Internet — and wait after the Electoral Board approves the petition and returns all the documentation to them. Once that documentation has been received, deposit the vote in the corresponding consulate, or do it by mail. Therefore, the voting in favour, the vote that exists right now, is a system with a huge bureaucratic burden which requires a great deal of effort for those who want to exercise their right to vote and which depends beyond the functioning of the postal services of their respective countries. I don't want to tell you what has to happen to those who live in countries like Cuba or Venezuela. [[Laughter]] From the very first moment, the protests of migrants have continued: by the succession of procedures to be met, by the adjustment of deadlines, or by the fact that the paperwork does not arrive in time in those countries where mail systems operate poorly, and also by all the procedures that must be put in place. In short, a hatred for anyone who wants to exercise this right. And what is the result of all this? Well, a drastic fall in the participation of the foreign vote since the 2011 reform. We're going to put some data on the table: in the general elections of 2008, in Spain voted 31.88 percent of citizens entitled to vote in CERA; in the general elections of 2016, 8.78 percent applied for the vote, and of them only 6.30 percent voted. That's in Spain, but in Galicia the data are even more worrying: in 2009 the vote was 22.6 percent — in autonomics — and in 2016 — in general — was 2.41 percent. If this data is alarming, it is also worrying that there were nine thousand Greeks who applied for the vote, and that in the end their vote was not reached, they could not exercise that right. In view of the data, it is obvious that the voting system is not fulfilling its role: it favours further abstention from the slowness and complexity of the process, even often because of the material impossibility of voting. That's why, despite the 2011 reform, the community continues to demand that a regulation be adapted as much as possible to the one we have here in Spain, and that it can also facilitate that intervention, that participation in the election of its representatives, its representatives of a country in which they are very integrated, in which they have emotional, personal, territorial, social, cultural, economic ties to those who do not want to bond with each other; links that are good for them and for us and that are playing a decisive role also in the demographic challenge. The Galician People's Party has repeatedly expressed its commitment to the reform of LOREX towards a system of agile, safe and transparent vote, and we will explain a little what our proposal would be. First of all, to remove the voting system, and to move on to a system of storage in the furnace as a generalized system, and for this reason it would have to increase, with regard to what is currently, the depositary of votes, the places of voting, including the consulates, with the honorary consulates, the regional houses, the houses of Spain, the Cervantes Institute or other centres authorized and attended by officials of the Spanish State. The voters from the outside must have the ballots that reach them there, and they can have different systems — they should see —. We propose that it may be, for example, through the parties, by downloading them from the Internet, by distributing them to the polling tables themselves, or by being able to use blank papers where everyone can write their choice. The vote would be kept by mail, the vote begged as it is right now, that is, for those cases of material impossibility to do it differently. For example, for those who are sick, disabled, traveling or those who live in countries where distances are very long and there is a small community in an isolated area, which would not allow them to place a furnace. Therefore, the deletion of the requested vote as a generalized system. Secondly, documentation should be made available to countries abroad in greater advance. Thirdly, to extend the voting days, which are currently three, by going to seven, and taking two weekends, so that in this way they could go to vote more easily on those people who are working and, in addition, could do it on a weekend or the other. And fourthly, constantly updating the CERA, because, in this way, we would never leave people who would be in a position to vote, but, by not updating the census, they can't do it. These are the details of our proposal, but you have already read it and you see that the non-law proposal we are debating today and that we are presenting today is much more generic. We do so this way because it seems to us that, on such an important issue as this, we should seek the greatest consensus; a consensus that can be a minimum proposal, which is the elimination of the voted vote, and hence the simplification of the voting system, and the transparency and absolute guarantees for the voters. So today, all of us, and all of you, have the opportunity to tell the Galicians of the diaspora and the Greeks that we live in Galicia that system, which electoral model we prefer: a system that favors participation and security in electoral processes, eliminating the vote begged but giving all guarantees, or a model that you cannot, often, and always disables, in practice, the vote of the diaspora, which is what is happening with the current law after the reform of 2011, or even the return to an expired system that favours fraud — the previous year 2011. The People's Group has always had it, and it's very clear. We want a democratic system that promotes participation and security in electoral processes. That is our proposal, and we hope to count on the support of all the groups in something that is decisive for all the Greeks and the Greeks.

And this should call us to a serene but necessary reflection on the content of this document. By the way, we don't see that there were abstentions at the board of directors, so could we. So, we can see that when we talk about the service transport demand, we constantly talk about it as a user-oriented service, as it could not, of course, be otherwise, but in drafting those lines we find ourselves with paragraphs that give us serious doubts about that orientation. I suppose everyone who has a direct responsibility to speak of this non-law proposal knows what that document says. And he speaks in particular of the fact that, for the implementation of low demand management forms, the maximum expansion of the supply of public transport services in rural areas will be promoted; that these demand services will be an essential element as a formula for streamlining resources in areas of high population dispersion; and that in these areas user-oriented services will be designed to communicate with the operator with a centralized system. It lays down three modes of transport which I do not have time to detail, but the master lines themselves give as much as possible the establishment of routes in fixed times being the user — which is very relevant — who demands transport and who needs to adapt to the established service. What you have to adapt to seems more or less reasonable, which requests the service is already less. Why? Because these master lines provide that it is expected that all stops planned for the full service will be carried out along the established itinerary, which have been previously requested and confirmed with the request for requests, it will not be necessary to stop in those cases where no user intends to access or abandon the vehicle. But it is said that it is expected that you can give access to user persons who are found at the stops and did not make the request of the previous service always and when the number of reserved squares is kept empty for the people who requested it. I mean, we can find ourselves in the reality that there are people -- it's not very hard to imagine that older people -- waiting for a bus to pass to them to go to a medical consultation, to go to a hospital, to just go to a market or a supermarket to supply themselves, to see them pass by the bus without stopping because they haven't done the demand or because that bus at that time has all the complete squares because it's already demanded before and it's not required the concessionaire to put on a more capable vehicle. For that reason, and not for the other reason, our parliamentary group has tabled this non-law proposal, in which we ask that the Galician Council effectively power and carry out the transport services on demand in order to ensure the mobility of people in the most neglected rural areas. I think it costs nobody much work to imagine that in these scattered areas, with very little population, besides that population is advanced in age, which makes it very difficult to access new technologies that will be necessary to interact with that platform.
